UTM Link Builder
Page Title (meta title): UTM Builder: Free Campaign Link Generator
Meta Description: Build a UTM builder link in seconds. Add utm_source, utm_medium and utm_campaign tags to any URL, then track the result in Google Analytics for free.
H1 Heading (on-page): UTM Builder
Short Description (on-page, under the icon next to the H1): Turn any link into a tagged campaign URL. Add your source, medium and campaign values with the UTM builder below, then copy the finished link straight into your next email, ad or social post.
Primary keyword: utm builder (12,100/mo, KD 64) · Secondary keyword: utm generator (3,600/mo, KD 49)
About UTM Link Builder
Lead paragraph: A UTM builder takes a plain link and adds a handful of extra parameters to the end of it, so when someone clicks through, your analytics tool knows exactly where that visit came from. This UTM generator builds that link for you in the browser, no spreadsheet or memory of the naming convention required.
Left column
1. Every UTM link is made up of a few parts: utm_source tells you where the click came from (newsletter, facebook, google), utm_medium tells you the channel (email, cpc, social), and utm_campaign names the specific push you're running (spring-sale, product-launch). Two more fields, utm_term and utm_content, are optional and mostly useful for paid search or testing different versions of the same ad.
2. Without tagging, Google Analytics and most other platforms lump all your traffic into generic buckets like "referral" or "direct", and you lose the ability to tell which campaign actually drove the result. A consistent UTM builder habit is what turns a vague sense of "social is working" into a real answer, like knowing your Tuesday newsletter link outperformed the Friday one by three to one.
Right column
1. Fill in the destination URL and the three main fields, and the tool assembles everything into one clean link, properly encoded so spaces and special characters don't break it. There's a copy button next to the result so you're not selecting text by hand, and the fields stay filled in if you need to tweak one value and generate a slightly different version for another channel.
2. This UTM generator runs entirely in your browser, so the links you build aren't logged or stored anywhere. That matters if you're tagging URLs for a client project or an unannounced launch. Use it as often as you need for email campaigns, paid ads, social posts or affiliate links, there's no limit and nothing to sign up for.
FAQs
1. What's the difference between a UTM builder and a UTM generator? Nothing really, they're two names for the same thing: a tool that adds tracking parameters to a URL so your analytics platform can tell where a visitor came from. Both terms get used interchangeably across marketing teams.
2. Which UTM parameters are actually required? Google Analytics only needs three to register a source: utm_source, utm_medium and utm_campaign. The other two, utm_term and utm_content, are optional and mainly used in paid search or when you're running two versions of the same ad and want to tell them apart in your reports.
3. Will UTM parameters affect my SEO or break my page? No. UTM parameters are just query string additions to a URL, they don't change the page itself or how search engines index it. The one thing to watch is sharing tagged links as your canonical URL on your own site, since that can create duplicate-looking URLs. For social posts, ads and emails, tagging is completely safe.
4. Can I use a UTM builder for links other than Google Analytics? Yes. Most major analytics tools, including Google Analytics, Adobe Analytics and most marketing platforms like HubSpot or Mailchimp, read the same utm_ parameters. Build the link once here and it reports correctly across any platform that reads standard UTM tags.
5. Do spaces or special characters in my campaign name cause problems? They can if you type them in manually, since a raw space or symbol can break a URL. This tool encodes everything automatically when it builds the link, so you can type "spring sale 2026" naturally and it comes out correctly formatted in the final URL.
6. Should I use dashes or underscores in my UTM values? Dashes are the more common convention (spring-sale rather than spring_sale), mainly because Google Analytics treats values as case-sensitive and dashes read more cleanly in reports. Pick one style and stick with it across your team, consistency matters more than which symbol you choose.
Open Graph Preview Generator
Page Title (meta title): Open Graph Preview Generator: Check Your Link Card
Meta Description: Preview your Open Graph tags before you post. See exactly how your link's title, description and og:image will look on Facebook, X and LinkedIn.
H1 Heading (on-page): Open Graph Preview Generator
Short Description (on-page, under the icon next to the H1): Paste in a URL and see the og:image preview, title and description your link will show when it's shared on Facebook, X or LinkedIn, before you actually post it.
Primary keyword: open graph preview (480/mo, KD 28) · Secondary keyword: og image preview (140/mo, KD 23)
About Open Graph Preview Generator
Lead paragraph: An open graph preview shows you what a link looks like the moment someone shares it, the image, headline and short blurb that show up in the post instead of a bare URL. This tool reads your page's Open Graph tags and renders that card right here, so you can catch a missing image or an awkward title before it goes out to your actual audience.
Left column
1. Open Graph tags are a small set of meta tags, og:title, og:description and og:image mainly, that you add to a page's head section. Facebook, LinkedIn and most other platforms read these tags to build the preview card, and if a page doesn't have them, the platform just guesses, usually pulling the first image and heading it finds, which is rarely the result you want.
2. X (formerly Twitter) uses a slightly different but related set of tags, called Twitter Cards, though it falls back to Open Graph tags when those aren't present. That's why a link can look great on Facebook but show the wrong image on X, the two platforms aren't always reading the exact same source.
Right column
1. Drop in any URL and the tool fetches the page's meta tags and lays them out the way they'd actually appear in a social feed, including the og image preview at the right aspect ratio. If something's missing, blank or the wrong size, you'll see it immediately instead of finding out after a post is already live.
2. This is especially useful right before a launch, a blog post goes out, or you've just redesigned a page and aren't sure the share card updated. Check it here first, fix the tags if something's off, and you'll avoid the broken-looking grey box or stretched image that undermines an otherwise good post.
FAQs
1. Why does my link show no image when I share it? Usually because the page is missing an og:image tag, or the image it points to is broken, too small, or blocked from being crawled. Some platforms also cache an old preview from the first time a link was shared, so a fix on your end might not show up until you force a re-scrape through that platform's own debugging tool.
2. What size should my og:image be? The common recommendation is 1200 by 630 pixels, which gives a roughly 1.91:1 ratio that displays cleanly on Facebook, LinkedIn and X without cropping awkwardly. Keep the file under about 5MB and use a readable image, since it often gets shown fairly small in a feed.
3. Does this tool change anything on my website? No, it only reads the meta tags already present on the page you enter and shows you what they produce. If you want to change the actual preview, you'll need to edit the og:title, og:description or og:image tags in your site's code or CMS.
4. Why does Facebook show an old image even after I updated it? Facebook caches link previews, so if a link's already been shared once, it may keep showing the old card until the cache clears. You can force a refresh using Facebook's own Sharing Debugger tool, which re-scrapes the page and updates the cached preview.
5. Is an Open Graph preview the same as a Twitter Card? They're related but not identical. Twitter Cards are X's own meta tag format, but X reads Open Graph tags as a fallback when Twitter Card tags aren't present. Having solid og:title, og:description and og:image tags covers you on most platforms, including X, even without separate Twitter Card tags.
6. Do I need Open Graph tags on every page of my site? It's worth having them at least on pages people are likely to share, blog posts, product pages, landing pages. A homepage benefits too, since it's often the first link someone pastes into a group chat or a social post.

H1 Heading (on-page): Keyword Density Checker
Short Description (on-page, under the icon next to the H1): Paste in your content and this keyword density checker counts how often each word and phrase appears, so you can see if a keyword is underused or stuffed before you publish.
Primary keyword: keyword density checker (1,000/mo, KD 29) · Secondary keyword: keyword density calculator (110/mo, KD 13)
About Keyword Density Checker
Lead paragraph: Keyword density is simply how often a word or phrase shows up in a piece of content, shown as a percentage of the total word count. This keyword density checker counts every instance for you and ranks the results, which is a faster way to catch an overused phrase than rereading the whole page yourself.
Left column
1. There's no official "correct" density that guarantees a better ranking, and search engines haven't used a fixed density target in years. What actually matters is whether a keyword reads naturally in context. A keyword density calculator is more useful as a sanity check than a target to hit exactly, it tells you when something looks off rather than telling you the one right number.
2. The classic problem this catches is keyword stuffing, repeating a phrase so often that the writing starts to sound robotic and reads worse for an actual visitor. Modern search engines are good at detecting this and it can hurt rankings rather than help them, so a tool that flags an unusually high repeat count is doing you a favor before you hit publish.
Right column
1. Paste in a draft, a product description or an existing page's text, and the tool breaks down single words as well as two and three word phrases, each with a count and a percentage of the total. That makes it easy to see not just that you used "running shoes" a lot, but exactly how many times, and whether a related phrase barely shows up at all.
2. It's useful both ways: catching a phrase you've leaned on too heavily, and spotting a secondary or related keyword you meant to include but forgot. Run a quick check before you publish a blog post, submit a product page, or hand a draft off to a client, it takes a few seconds and the whole thing happens in your browser.
FAQs
1. What's a good keyword density percentage? There's no single correct number, and search engines don't rank pages based on hitting a specific percentage. As a rough guide, most well-written content naturally lands somewhere between 1 and 2 percent for a primary keyword. Anything noticeably higher is worth a second read to check it still sounds natural.
2. Can keyword stuffing get my page penalized? It can hurt rather than help. Search engines are built to recognize unnaturally repetitive text, and content that reads like it was written for an algorithm instead of a person tends to rank worse, not better. Writing naturally and checking density afterward is a safer approach than writing toward a target percentage.
3. Does this tool check density for phrases, not just single words? Yes, it analyzes single words along with two and three word phrases, so a phrase like "best running shoes" gets counted as its own entry, separate from how often "running" or "shoes" appear individually.
4. Should I count common words like "the" or "and" in my density check? Those words will always show up with high counts since they're a normal part of sentence structure, so they're not useful signals on their own. Focus on the keywords and phrases that are actually relevant to the topic of your content rather than function words.
5. Is keyword density still relevant for SEO in 2026? It matters less as a direct ranking factor than it used to, since modern search engines focus more on topic relevance and natural language than exact repeat counts. It's still a useful proofreading check though, mainly for catching accidental overuse or confirming you've actually mentioned your target topic enough.
6. Can I check a competitor's page with this tool? You can paste in any text, including a competitor's published content, to see how they've used keywords on a page that's already ranking. Just keep in mind you're seeing their current content, not necessarily the exact version that helped them rank originally.
Meta Description Length Checker
Page Title (meta title): Meta Description Checker: Avoid Google Truncation
Meta Description: Check your title and meta description length before Google cuts them off. This meta description checker shows a live SERP preview as you type.
H1 Heading (on-page): Meta Description Checker
Short Description (on-page, under the icon next to the H1): Type or paste your title and meta description below and this checker shows you a live Google search preview, so you can see exactly where Google is likely to cut it off.
Primary keyword: meta description checker (480/mo, KD 24) · Secondary keyword: meta description length checker (260/mo, KD 5)
About Meta Description Length Checker
Lead paragraph: Google doesn't go by a strict character count when it decides how much of your title and meta description to show, it measures pixel width, which means a title full of wide letters like "w" and "m" gets cut off sooner than one with narrow letters like "i" and "l". This meta description checker estimates that width for you and shows a live preview of your snippet, so you're not guessing based on character count alone.
Left column
1. As a rough guide, titles tend to display fully up to around 60 characters and meta descriptions up to around 155 to 160 characters, though both can run a little longer or shorter depending on the actual letters used and whether Google decides to rewrite your snippet entirely, which it sometimes does regardless of length.
2. A meta title checker matters because a truncated title often cuts off mid-word or mid-phrase, which looks unfinished and can cost you clicks even when the page itself ranks well. The same goes for a description that gets chopped off right before the sentence makes its point, the reader never sees the reason to click.
Right column
1. Paste in your draft title and description and watch the live preview update as you edit, with a clear marker showing roughly where Google is likely to truncate. That makes it easy to trim a sentence back by a few words rather than publishing blind and checking Google's actual search results days later.
2. This is worth running on every new page before it goes live, not just occasionally. A blog post, a product page, a landing page, anywhere you've written a title and description by hand is worth a quick check here first, since a snippet that gets cut off awkwardly is an easy, avoidable way to lose clicks.
FAQs
1. What's the ideal meta description length? Aim for roughly 150 to 160 characters as a safe target. Google measures by pixel width rather than a strict character count, so the exact cutoff shifts slightly depending on which letters you use, but staying under 160 characters keeps you safe in almost every case.
2. Why does Google sometimes ignore my meta description and show different text? Google often rewrites the snippet using text pulled from the page itself when it decides your written description doesn't match the search query well, or when it's missing, too short, or too generic. Writing a specific, relevant description for each page's main topic reduces how often this happens, though it can't guarantee Google never rewrites it.
3. Is there an exact character limit, or does it vary? It varies, because Google truncates based on pixel width, not character count. A title made up of wide letters gets cut off sooner than a shorter-looking title full of narrow letters. That's why this checker shows a visual preview instead of just a number, since the number alone doesn't tell the whole story.
4. Does title length affect SEO rankings directly? Length itself isn't a ranking factor, but a truncated title can hurt your click-through rate, which is something search engines do pay attention to over time. A clear, complete title that displays fully tends to perform better than one that gets cut off mid-thought, even if both are technically the same length in characters.
5. Should my meta description include my target keyword? It's a good idea where it fits naturally, since Google bolds matching terms in the snippet when they appear in the search query, which can make your result stand out. Don't force it in awkwardly though, a description that reads naturally and makes someone want to click matters more than keyword placement alone.
6. Can I check multiple pages at once? This tool checks one title and description at a time so you can see a precise, focused preview for each page. For a larger site audit across many pages at once, you'd want a crawling tool instead, this one is built for getting a single page's snippet exactly right before you publish it.
X/Twitter Thread Splitter
Page Title (meta title): Twitter Thread Maker: Split Text Into Tweets
Meta Description: Paste a long post and this thread maker splits it into numbered, tweet-sized sections ready to post on X, one click to copy each part.
H1 Heading (on-page): Twitter Thread Maker
Short Description (on-page, under the icon next to the H1): Paste in a long piece of writing and this thread maker breaks it into numbered, properly sized posts for X, each one ready to copy and paste in order.
Primary keyword: twitter thread maker (110/mo, KD 4). Note: demand for this tool's exact function is genuinely low across every variant tested, so this is the best realistic target, not a high-volume term.
About X/Twitter Thread Splitter
Lead paragraph: X still caps a single post at 280 characters for most accounts, which means any longer piece of writing, a story, an explanation, a set of tips, has to be broken up manually into a thread. This thread maker does that splitting for you, breaking your text into clean, numbered sections sized to fit the limit instead of you counting characters by hand.
Left column
1. Splitting a long post by hand usually means counting characters, cutting a sentence awkwardly in half, or losing track of which numbered part comes next while you're copying each one over. It's a small task that still eats real time when you're trying to post something while an idea is fresh, or right after a launch when every minute matters.
2. A good thread needs more than just a character cutoff too, breaking mid-sentence looks sloppy and makes the thread harder to follow. This tool tries to break at natural points like the end of a sentence where it can, so each post in the thread reads as a complete thought rather than trailing off.
Right column
1. Paste your full text in, and the tool splits it into individual posts, each one numbered so your readers (and you) can keep track of where they are in the thread. Every section comes with its own copy button, so you can paste them into X one at a time without second-guessing the order.
2. This works for more than just X. The same split, numbered text works fine for a LinkedIn post broken into comments, a long caption split across an Instagram carousel, or any other platform with its own character limit. Use it any time you've written something longer than one post can hold and don't want to do the math yourself.
FAQs
1. How many characters can one X post actually hold? Standard accounts get 280 characters per post. Accounts with X Premium can post significantly longer single posts, but splitting into a thread is still common even for those accounts, since a numbered thread is often easier for people to read and engage with one point at a time.
2. Does this tool post directly to X for me? No, it only splits your text into numbered sections. You copy each part and paste it into X yourself, which also gives you a chance to review each post before it goes out rather than auto-publishing something you haven't double-checked.
3. Will it cut off in the middle of a sentence? It tries not to. The tool looks for natural breaking points like the end of a sentence or a paragraph rather than just chopping text at exactly 280 characters, so each post in the thread should read as a complete thought wherever your original writing allows for that.
4. Can I use this for platforms other than X? Yes. The same numbered, character-limited output works for splitting a long LinkedIn post into comments, breaking up an Instagram caption, or any other place where you're working within a character limit and want clean, numbered sections.
5. Does the tool add the thread numbering automatically? Yes, each section is numbered (1/, 2/, 3/ and so on) by default, which is the common convention for X threads and helps readers follow along, especially if they land on a post in the middle of the thread rather than the first one.
6. Is there a limit to how much text I can split at once? No hard limit, you can paste in a long article or a full set of notes and it'll break the whole thing into as many numbered posts as needed. Everything runs in your browser, so there's no file size cap or server timeout to worry about either.
